========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because I’ve started doing things that honestly scare me a little. Or more than a little, I guess. I’m not someone who usually acts impulsively—I’m a computer systems analyst, so my whole life is kind of built around planning, checking things, making sure systems don’t fail. But lately, when I get overwhelmed, I’ll just… leave. Like in the middle of a deployment, I’ve walked out. Not just stepped away for coffee—I mean I’ve actually left when I knew I was supposed to be there.

And then there are these nights where I get in the car and drive around really late with no plan, no destination, just driving. I know that sounds maybe not that dramatic, but for me it is. It feels reckless, and afterward I think, “What am I doing?” This is not how I’ve ever handled stress before, and it’s getting to the point where I don’t trust my own reactions. That’s why I’m here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. It’s more like it just breaks in.

It happens out of nowhere sometimes, but there are certain places or situations that seem to set it off more. That conference area at work is one of them. I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.

And if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.

But lately, when I get overwhelmed, I’ll just… leave. Like in the middle of a deployment, I’ve walked out. Not just stepped away for coffee—I mean I’ve actually left when I knew I was supposed to be there.\n\nAnd then there are these nights where I get in the car and drive around really late with no plan, no destination, just driving. I know that sounds maybe not that dramatic, but for me it is. It feels reckless, and afterward I think, “What am I doing?” This is not how I’ve ever handled stress before, and it’s getting to the point where I don’t trust my own reactions. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.

At work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.

And conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.

then when I finally do fall asleep I wake up tense from dreams or from some small noise and I’m exhausted before work even starts.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Out of nowhere, especially in that conference area or when someone raises their voice on a call, I’ll get hit with images and sounds from that night so strongly that I lose track of what I was saying.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I take the long way around the building, avoid that floor whenever I can, and I shut down conversations the moment people start talking about the contractor or the cutover.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I can remember some parts of what happened very clearly, like the contractor’s face and the noise, but there’s a chunk in the middle where I know I was there and I just can’t pull it back.\n- key: S074\n  name: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\n  description: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).\n  manifestation: Even on quiet days I feel this mix of fear and shame sitting in my chest, like I should have handled everything better and like something could still happen if I let my guard down.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: My husband will hug me or tell me he’s proud of me, and I know I love him in my head, but the warm feeling I used to get just doesn’t come through.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n  description: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.\n  manifestation: I came in because I’ve started doing things that scare me, like walking out in the middle of deployments or driving around late at night with no plan, and that is not how I’ve ever handled stress before.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a door slams, someone comes up behind my chair, or a phone suddenly rings, I jerk so hard my hands shake and it can take me several minutes to look normal again.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My performance review was the first one where I was told I’m missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and making the team work around me, and at home my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that late-night cutover incident a little over a year ago, about 13 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S078\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve started doing things that honestly scare me a little. Or more than a little, I guess. I’m not someone who usually acts impulsively—I’m a computer systems analyst, so my whole life is kind of built around planning, checking things, making sure systems don’t fail. But lately, when I get overwhelmed, I’ll just… leave. Like in the middle of a deployment, I’ve walked out. Not just stepped away for coffee—I mean I’ve actually left when I knew I was supposed to be there.\n\nAnd then there are these nights where I get in the car and drive around really late with no plan, no destination, just driving. I know that sounds maybe not that dramatic, but for me it is. It feels reckless, and afterward I think, “What am I doing?” This is not how I’ve ever handled stress before, and it’s getting to the point where I don’t trust my own reactions. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. It’s more like it just breaks in.\n\nIt happens out of nowhere sometimes, but there are certain places or situations that seem to set it off more. That conference area at work is one of them. I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.\n\nAnd if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble sleeping?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Most nights, honestly.

I’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.

And when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.

By the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.

hard my hands shake and it can take me several minutes to look normal again.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My performance review was the first one where I was told I’m missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and making the team work around me, and at home my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that late-night cutover incident a little over a year ago, about 13 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S078\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. It’s more like it just breaks in.\n\nIt happens out of nowhere sometimes, but there are certain places or situations that seem to set it off more. That conference area at work is one of them. I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.\n\nAnd if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much so.

It’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.

The phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.

hard my hands shake and it can take me several minutes to look normal again.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My performance review was the first one where I was told I’m missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and making the team work around me, and at home my husband says it feels like I’m barely present anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that late-night cutover incident a little over a year ago, about 13 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S078\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes. It has, and I think that’s the part that’s been hardest to admit, because for a while I kept telling myself I was still functioning. I was showing up to work, I was answering emails, I was doing enough to look like I was keeping it together. But then my performance review happened, and it was the first one where the feedback wasn’t just, you know, “tighten up documentation” or “communicate earlier.” It was more pointed than that.

They told me I’ve been missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Hearing it said that plainly was… awful. Because I knew I’d been ducking out of things or avoiding certain discussions, but I hadn’t really let myself think about how much extra strain that put on other people. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. But the truth is, they weren’t wrong.

And at home, my husband has said it feels like I’m barely present anymore. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. He’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I haven’t really heard half of what he said, or I’m just giving these automatic answers. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. More than I wanted to see.

Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. Maybe not in a clean, logical way, but I feel it.

I keep going back over it in my head, thinking I should have handled things differently. Like I should have noticed something sooner, or said something differently, or reacted faster, or… I don’t know. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.

And it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Even on quiet days, when everything is technically fine, I still feel like I’m carrying it around. Like if I relax too much or stop paying attention, something could happen again. So yes, I feel responsible in this constant background way, like I should have done better, and I can’t seem to convince myself otherwise.

I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has, and I think that’s the part that’s been hardest to admit, because for a while I kept telling myself I was still functioning. I was showing up to work, I was answering emails, I was doing enough to look like I was keeping it together. But then my performance review happened, and it was the first one where the feedback wasn’t just, you know, “tighten up documentation” or “communicate earlier.” It was more pointed than that.\n\nThey told me I’ve been missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Hearing it said that plainly was… awful. Because I knew I’d been ducking out of things or avoiding certain discussions, but I hadn’t really let myself think about how much extra strain that put on other people. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. But the truth is, they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said it feels like I’m barely present anymore. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. He’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I haven’t really heard half of what he said, or I’m just giving these automatic answers. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. More than I wanted to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible for what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not in a clean, logical way, but I feel it.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, thinking I should have handled things differently. Like I should have noticed something sooner, or said something differently, or reacted faster, or… I don’t know. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Even on quiet days, when everything is technically fine, I still feel like I’m carrying it around. Like if I relax too much or stop paying attention, something could happen again. So yes, I feel responsible in this constant background way, like I should have done better, and I can’t seem to convince myself otherwise.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. A lot, actually.

I used to be the kind of person who had something planned almost every night, or at least something I was looking forward to. Salsa class was a big one—I’d plan my week around it. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I still liked going because it was familiar and social and kind of grounding. I used to do yoga regularly too, and I loved having people over. I’d cook Guatemalan food and make way too much of it, and it felt like… I don’t know, like one of the ways I knew who I was.

Now I mostly cancel. Or I don’t make the plans in the first place because I already know I’m probably not going to follow through. People will text about getting together, and I’ll tell myself I’ll answer later, and then later turns into days. If I do say yes, I spend the whole day thinking of how to get out of it.

And then instead of doing any of those things, I just sit at home. I’ll scroll on my phone for an hour without even really absorbing anything, or I’ll put on a show and pretend I’m watching it, but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just passing time. That’s the part that scares me a little, because these weren’t random hobbies. They were things I genuinely used to love.

Not just stepped away for coffee—I mean I’ve actually left when I knew I was supposed to be there.\n\nAnd then there are these nights where I get in the car and drive around really late with no plan, no destination, just driving. I know that sounds maybe not that dramatic, but for me it is. It feels reckless, and afterward I think, “What am I doing?” This is not how I’ve ever handled stress before, and it’s getting to the point where I don’t trust my own reactions. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. It’s more like it just breaks in.\n\nIt happens out of nowhere sometimes, but there are certain places or situations that seem to set it off more. That conference area at work is one of them. I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.\n\nAnd if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has, and I think that’s the part that’s been hardest to admit, because for a while I kept telling myself I was still functioning. I was showing up to work, I was answering emails, I was doing enough to look like I was keeping it together. But then my performance review happened, and it was the first one where the feedback wasn’t just, you know, “tighten up documentation” or “communicate earlier.” It was more pointed than that.\n\nThey told me I’ve been missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Hearing it said that plainly was… awful. Because I knew I’d been ducking out of things or avoiding certain discussions, but I hadn’t really let myself think about how much extra strain that put on other people. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. But the truth is, they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said it feels like I’m barely present anymore. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. He’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I haven’t really heard half of what he said, or I’m just giving these automatic answers. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. More than I wanted to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible for what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not in a clean, logical way, but I feel it.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, thinking I should have handled things differently. Like I should have noticed something sooner, or said something differently, or reacted faster, or… I don’t know. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Even on quiet days, when everything is technically fine, I still feel like I’m carrying it around. Like if I relax too much or stop paying attention, something could happen again. So yes, I feel responsible in this constant background way, like I should have done better, and I can’t seem to convince myself otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the kind of person who had something planned almost every night, or at least something I was looking forward to. Salsa class was a big one—I’d plan my week around it. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I still liked going because it was familiar and social and kind of grounding. I used to do yoga regularly too, and I loved having people over. I’d cook Guatemalan food and make way too much of it, and it felt like… I don’t know, like one of the ways I knew who I was.\n\nNow I mostly cancel. Or I don’t make the plans in the first place because I already know I’m probably not going to follow through. People will text about getting together, and I’ll tell myself I’ll answer later, and then later turns into days. If I do say yes, I spend the whole day thinking of how to get out of it.\n\nAnd then instead of doing any of those things, I just sit at home. I’ll scroll on my phone for an hour without even really absorbing anything, or I’ll put on a show and pretend I’m watching it, but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just passing time. That’s the part that scares me a little, because these weren’t random hobbies. They were things I genuinely used to love.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I would describe it exactly that way, like I’m floating above myself or literally watching from outside my body. That sounds more specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere are times when I feel kind of… not fully connected, I guess? Like I’m going through the motions and I’m aware that I’m doing things, but it feels muted or far away. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t had a clear experience where I’m looking at myself from the outside, if that makes sense. It’s more that I feel distant from myself sometimes, but I don’t know how to separate that from just being overwhelmed or checked out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F43.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible for what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not in a clean, logical way, but I feel it.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, thinking I should have handled things differently. Like I should have noticed something sooner, or said something differently, or reacted faster, or… I don’t know. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Even on quiet days, when everything is technically fine, I still feel like I’m carrying it around. Like if I relax too much or stop paying attention, something could happen again. So yes, I feel responsible in this constant background way, like I should have done better, and I can’t seem to convince myself otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the kind of person who had something planned almost every night, or at least something I was looking forward to. Salsa class was a big one—I’d plan my week around it. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I still liked going because it was familiar and social and kind of grounding. I used to do yoga regularly too, and I loved having people over. I’d cook Guatemalan food and make way too much of it, and it felt like… I don’t know, like one of the ways I knew who I was.\n\nNow I mostly cancel. Or I don’t make the plans in the first place because I already know I’m probably not going to follow through. People will text about getting together, and I’ll tell myself I’ll answer later, and then later turns into days. If I do say yes, I spend the whole day thinking of how to get out of it.\n\nAnd then instead of doing any of those things, I just sit at home. I’ll scroll on my phone for an hour without even really absorbing anything, or I’ll put on a show and pretend I’m watching it, but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just passing time. That’s the part that scares me a little, because these weren’t random hobbies. They were things I genuinely used to love.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I would describe it exactly that way, like I’m floating above myself or literally watching from outside my body. That sounds more specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere are times when I feel kind of… not fully connected, I guess? Like I’m going through the motions and I’m aware that I’m doing things, but it feels muted or far away. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t had a clear experience where I’m looking at myself from the outside, if that makes sense. It’s more that I feel distant from myself sometimes, but I don’t know how to separate that from just being overwhelmed or checked out.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out, as if you're not fully aware of what's happening around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I’m trying to think of an example, and I’m not sure I can say yes in the way you’re asking.

I do have moments where I feel sort of… slowed down, or like I’m not taking things in very well. Like someone might be talking and I realize I missed part of what they said, but I don’t know if that’s being “in a daze” or just being distracted or tired or in my own head. It’s not like I completely lose awareness of where I am or what’s happening around me. I don’t think so, anyway.

Sometimes things feel a little blurred together, but I’m still there, if that makes sense. I can answer questions, I can keep doing whatever I’m doing. It’s more like my attention is not fully sharp. So… I guess I haven’t noticed anything dramatic like spacing out and not knowing what’s going on. If it happens, it’s subtle enough that I’m not totally sure what to call it.

But lately, when I get overwhelmed, I’ll just… leave. Like in the middle of a deployment, I’ve walked out. Not just stepped away for coffee—I mean I’ve actually left when I knew I was supposed to be there.\n\nAnd then there are these nights where I get in the car and drive around really late with no plan, no destination, just driving. I know that sounds maybe not that dramatic, but for me it is. It feels reckless, and afterward I think, “What am I doing?” This is not how I’ve ever handled stress before, and it’s getting to the point where I don’t trust my own reactions. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. It’s more like it just breaks in.\n\nIt happens out of nowhere sometimes, but there are certain places or situations that seem to set it off more. That conference area at work is one of them. I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.\n\nAnd if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has, and I think that’s the part that’s been hardest to admit, because for a while I kept telling myself I was still functioning. I was showing up to work, I was answering emails, I was doing enough to look like I was keeping it together. But then my performance review happened, and it was the first one where the feedback wasn’t just, you know, “tighten up documentation” or “communicate earlier.” It was more pointed than that.\n\nThey told me I’ve been missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Hearing it said that plainly was… awful. Because I knew I’d been ducking out of things or avoiding certain discussions, but I hadn’t really let myself think about how much extra strain that put on other people. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. But the truth is, they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said it feels like I’m barely present anymore. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. He’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I haven’t really heard half of what he said, or I’m just giving these automatic answers. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Yes. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me, because it’s not like the whole thing is missing. Some parts are almost too clear.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve started doing things that honestly scare me a little. Or more than a little, I guess. I’m not someone who usually acts impulsively—I’m a computer systems analyst, so my whole life is kind of built around planning, checking things, making sure systems don’t fail. But lately, when I get overwhelmed, I’ll just… leave. Like in the middle of a deployment, I’ve walked out. Not just stepped away for coffee—I mean I’ve actually left when I knew I was supposed to be there.\n\nAnd then there are these nights where I get in the car and drive around really late with no plan, no destination, just driving. I know that sounds maybe not that dramatic, but for me it is. It feels reckless, and afterward I think, “What am I doing?” This is not how I’ve ever handled stress before, and it’s getting to the point where I don’t trust my own reactions. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely. It’s not like I’m sitting there intentionally thinking about it or replaying it on purpose. It’s more like it just breaks in.\n\nIt happens out of nowhere sometimes, but there are certain places or situations that seem to set it off more. That conference area at work is one of them. I can be in the middle of explaining something totally normal—like walking through a system issue or answering a question—and suddenly I’ll get hit with these images and sounds from that night. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.\n\nAnd if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has, and I think that’s the part that’s been hardest to admit, because for a while I kept telling myself I was still functioning. I was showing up to work, I was answering emails, I was doing enough to look like I was keeping it together. But then my performance review happened, and it was the first one where the feedback wasn’t just, you know, “tighten up documentation” or “communicate earlier.” It was more pointed than that.\n\nThey told me I’ve been missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Hearing it said that plainly was… awful. Because I knew I’d been ducking out of things or avoiding certain discussions, but I hadn’t really let myself think about how much extra strain that put on other people. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. But the truth is, they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said it feels like I’m barely present anymore. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. He’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I haven’t really heard half of what he said, or I’m just giving these automatic answers. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. More than I wanted to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible for what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not in a clean, logical way, but I feel it.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, thinking I should have handled things differently. Like I should have noticed something sooner, or said something differently, or reacted faster, or… I don’t know. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Even on quiet days, when everything is technically fine, I still feel like I’m carrying it around. Like if I relax too much or stop paying attention, something could happen again. So yes, I feel responsible in this constant background way, like I should have done better, and I can’t seem to convince myself otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the kind of person who had something planned almost every night, or at least something I was looking forward to. Salsa class was a big one—I’d plan my week around it. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I still liked going because it was familiar and social and kind of grounding. I used to do yoga regularly too, and I loved having people over. I’d cook Guatemalan food and make way too much of it, and it felt like… I don’t know, like one of the ways I knew who I was.\n\nNow I mostly cancel. Or I don’t make the plans in the first place because I already know I’m probably not going to follow through. People will text about getting together, and I’ll tell myself I’ll answer later, and then later turns into days. If I do say yes, I spend the whole day thinking of how to get out of it.\n\nAnd then instead of doing any of those things, I just sit at home. I’ll scroll on my phone for an hour without even really absorbing anything, or I’ll put on a show and pretend I’m watching it, but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just passing time. That’s the part that scares me a little, because these weren’t random hobbies. They were things I genuinely used to love.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I would describe it exactly that way, like I’m floating above myself or literally watching from outside my body. That sounds more specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere are times when I feel kind of… not fully connected, I guess? Like I’m going through the motions and I’m aware that I’m doing things, but it feels muted or far away. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t had a clear experience where I’m looking at myself from the outside, if that makes sense. It’s more that I feel distant from myself sometimes, but I don’t know how to separate that from just being overwhelmed or checked out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out, as if you're not fully aware of what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of an example, and I’m not sure I can say yes in the way you’re asking.\n\nI do have moments where I feel sort of… slowed down, or like I’m not taking things in very well. It’s not like I completely lose awareness of where I am or what’s happening around me. I don’t think so, anyway.\n\nSometimes things feel a little blurred together, but I’m still there, if that makes sense. I can answer questions, I can keep doing whatever I’m doing. It’s more like my attention is not fully sharp. So… I guess I haven’t noticed anything dramatic like spacing out and not knowing what’s going on. It’s not just a thought like, “Oh, I remember that.” It feels much more immediate than that, like my brain just yanks me back there for a second.\n\nAnd if someone raises their voice on a call, even if they’re not yelling at me specifically, that can do it too. I’ll hear the tone shift and then I’m not really tracking the conversation anymore. I’ll lose the thread of what I was saying, which is embarrassing because I’m usually very precise at work. I’ll be mid-sentence and then just… blank, because these sounds or flashes are suddenly taking up all the space in my head. Then I’m trying to act normal and catch up, but inside I’m kind of scrambling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because of these intrusive images and sounds?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started taking these ridiculous routes around the building just to avoid going past certain areas. Like, I’ll take the long way around even if it makes no practical sense, because I don’t want to go near that floor if I can help it. I’ll tell myself it’s just because I need the steps or because I’m going to stop by someone’s desk, but really I know what I’m doing. I’m avoiding it.\n\nAnd conversations too. If people start talking about the contractor or the cutover, I just… I shut it down. I change the subject, or I suddenly need to get back to my desk, or I say we should take it offline even when there’s no real reason. I can feel myself getting tense as soon as those topics come up, and I don’t want to hear the details or the names or have everyone start rehashing it. I just want it to stop before it gets into my head again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll go to bed tired, like physically tired, and I think, “Okay, I should be able to sleep,” but then as soon as it’s quiet my mind starts pulling up pieces of what happened at the office. Not the whole thing in order, necessarily—just fragments. A sound, or someone’s face, or this one moment I keep circling back to even when I’m trying not to. I’ll turn over and tell myself to stop thinking about it, but that almost makes it worse because then I’m just lying there aware that I’m not sleeping.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up tense, like my whole body is braced. Sometimes it’s from a dream, sometimes it’s just some small noise in the house or outside and I’m suddenly awake, heart going, trying to figure out what it was. Then I look at the clock and start doing the math—how many hours until I have to be up for work—and that makes me more frustrated.\n\nBy the time morning comes, I feel like I’m already behind before the day has even started. Like I haven’t had any real reset overnight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it’s not subtle. If a door slams somewhere near me, or if someone walks up behind my chair at work and I don’t hear them coming, I physically jump. Not just a little flinch—I mean I jerk so hard that sometimes my hands shake afterward. If I’m holding a pen or my mouse, I’ll fumble with it, and then I’m immediately trying to cover it up like, “Oh, you startled me,” in this joking voice, but inside I’m still trying to get my body to calm down.\n\nThe phone ringing suddenly can do it too, especially if the room is quiet. It’s like my whole system reacts before I’ve even had time to understand what the sound is. And then I feel ridiculous because it’s just a door, or it’s just someone saying my name, or it’s just a phone. But even once I know that, my body doesn’t catch up right away. It can take several minutes before I feel like I look normal again—before my hands stop shaking and my face doesn’t feel so tense.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms or behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yes. It has, and I think that’s the part that’s been hardest to admit, because for a while I kept telling myself I was still functioning. I was showing up to work, I was answering emails, I was doing enough to look like I was keeping it together. But then my performance review happened, and it was the first one where the feedback wasn’t just, you know, “tighten up documentation” or “communicate earlier.” It was more pointed than that.\n\nThey told me I’ve been missing key handoffs, leaving meetings abruptly, and basically making the team work around me. Hearing it said that plainly was… awful. Because I knew I’d been ducking out of things or avoiding certain discussions, but I hadn’t really let myself think about how much extra strain that put on other people. I felt embarrassed, and also defensive at first, like I wanted to explain that I’m not trying to be difficult. But the truth is, they weren’t wrong.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said it feels like I’m barely present anymore. Not that I’m being cruel or starting fights or anything like that, but that I’m physically there and mentally somewhere else. He’ll be talking to me and I’ll realize I haven’t really heard half of what he said, or I’m just giving these automatic answers. I think he feels shut out, and I don’t know how to fix that without getting into things I don’t even want to talk about. So yes, it’s affecting people around me. More than I wanted to see.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible for what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Maybe not in a clean, logical way, but I feel it.\n\nI keep going back over it in my head, thinking I should have handled things differently. Like I should have noticed something sooner, or said something differently, or reacted faster, or… I don’t know. Even when part of me knows I didn’t have control over everything, there’s still this feeling sitting in my chest, this awful mix of fear and shame, like I failed some test I didn’t even know I was taking.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away just because nothing is happening right now. Even on quiet days, when everything is technically fine, I still feel like I’m carrying it around. Like if I relax too much or stop paying attention, something could happen again. So yes, I feel responsible in this constant background way, like I should have done better, and I can’t seem to convince myself otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot, actually.\n\nI used to be the kind of person who had something planned almost every night, or at least something I was looking forward to. Salsa class was a big one—I’d plan my week around it. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I still liked going because it was familiar and social and kind of grounding. I used to do yoga regularly too, and I loved having people over. I’d cook Guatemalan food and make way too much of it, and it felt like… I don’t know, like one of the ways I knew who I was.\n\nNow I mostly cancel. Or I don’t make the plans in the first place because I already know I’m probably not going to follow through. People will text about getting together, and I’ll tell myself I’ll answer later, and then later turns into days. If I do say yes, I spend the whole day thinking of how to get out of it.\n\nAnd then instead of doing any of those things, I just sit at home. I’ll scroll on my phone for an hour without even really absorbing anything, or I’ll put on a show and pretend I’m watching it, but I’m not really enjoying it. It’s more like I’m just passing time. That’s the part that scares me a little, because these weren’t random hobbies. They were things I genuinely used to love.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I would describe it exactly that way, like I’m floating above myself or literally watching from outside my body. That sounds more specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere are times when I feel kind of… not fully connected, I guess? Like I’m going through the motions and I’m aware that I’m doing things, but it feels muted or far away. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I haven’t had a clear experience where I’m looking at myself from the outside, if that makes sense. It’s more that I feel distant from myself sometimes, but I don’t know how to separate that from just being overwhelmed or checked out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out, as if you're not fully aware of what's happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m trying to think of an example, and I’m not sure I can say yes in the way you’re asking.\n\nI do have moments where I feel sort of… slowed down, or like I’m not taking things in very well. Like someone might be talking and I realize I missed part of what they said, but I don’t know if that’s being “in a daze” or just being distracted or tired or in my own head. It’s not like I completely lose awareness of where I am or what’s happening around me. I don’t think so, anyway.\n\nSometimes things feel a little blurred together, but I’m still there, if that makes sense. I can answer questions, I can keep doing whatever I’m doing. It’s more like my attention is not fully sharp. So… I guess I haven’t noticed anything dramatic like spacing out and not knowing what’s going on. If it happens, it’s subtle enough that I’m not totally sure what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you can't recall important aspects of the traumatic event?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me, because it’s not like the whole thing is missing. Some parts are almost too clear.\n\nI can remember the contractor’s face really sharply, like certain details of his expression, and I remember the noise — that part comes back in a way that feels very vivid. But then there’s this section in the middle where I know I was there, I know things happened, but when I try to put it in order or remember exactly what happened next, it’s just… blank, or kind of unreachable.\n\nIt’s not like I forgot because it was unimportant. It feels important. I just can’t pull it back, and the more I try, the more frustrating it gets. I’ll have the beginning and then a later part, but the middle is like someone cut it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that there are parts of the traumatic event that you cannot remember at all?\n\nPatient: Yes. There are parts I can’t remember at all, and that’s the strange part, because it isn’t like the whole thing is foggy. Some pieces are almost painfully clear — like I can picture the contractor’s face, and I can still remember the noise in this really sharp way.\n\nBut then there’s this chunk in the middle where… I know I was there. I know something happened in that time. It’s not like I left or wasn’t paying attention. But when I try to bring it back, there’s just nothing solid there. I can sort of remember before it, and then I remember after it, but that middle part is just blank, like I can’t get to it no matter how hard I try. It bothers me because it feels important, and I feel like I should be able to remember it, but I can’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like someone might be talking and I realize I missed part of what they said, but I don’t know if that’s being “in a daze” or just being distracted or tired or in my own head. It’s not like I completely lose awareness of where I am or what’s happening around me. I don’t think so, anyway.\n\nSometimes things feel a little blurred together, but I’m still there, if that makes sense. I can answer questions, I can keep doing whatever I’m doing. It’s more like my attention is not fully sharp. So… I guess I haven’t noticed anything dramatic like spacing out and not knowing what’s going on. If it happens, it’s subtle enough that I’m not totally sure what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you can't recall important aspects of the traumatic event?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me, because it’s not like the whole thing is missing. Some parts are almost too clear.\n\nI can remember the contractor’s face really sharply, like certain details of his expression, and I remember the noise — that part comes back in a way that feels very vivid. But then there’s this section in the middle where I know I was there, I know things happened, but when I try to put it in order or remember exactly what happened next, it’s just… blank, or kind of unreachable.\n\nIt’s not like I forgot because it was unimportant. It feels important. I just can’t pull it back, and the more I try, the more frustrating it gets. I’ll have the beginning and then a later part, but the middle is like someone cut it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that there are parts of the traumatic event that you cannot remember at all?\n\nPatient: Yes. There are parts I can’t remember at all, and that’s the strange part, because it isn’t like the whole thing is foggy. Some pieces are almost painfully clear — like I can picture the contractor’s face, and I can still remember the noise in this really sharp way.\n\nBut then there’s this chunk in the middle where… I know I was there. I know something happened in that time. It’s not like I left or wasn’t paying attention. But when I try to bring it back, there’s just nothing solid there. I can sort of remember before it, and then I remember after it, but that middle part is just blank, like I can’t get to it no matter how hard I try. It bothers me because it feels important, and I feel like I should be able to remember it, but I can’t.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're disconnected from your body or your surroundings?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.
